BLUFFDALE CITY                                                                                Mayor Natalie Hall
CITY COUNCIL &                                                                                Councilmember Wendy Aston
LOCAL BUILDING AUTHORITY                                                                      Councilmember Traci Crockett
                                                                                              Councilmember Jeff Gaston
COMBINED MEETING AGENDA
                                                                                              Councilmember Mark Hales
City Council Chambers                                                                         Councilmember Dave Kallas
Wednesday, May 10, 2023, at 6:00 p.m.

Notice is hereby given that the Bluffdale City Council & Local Building Authority will hold a combined meeting on Wednesday,
May 10, 2023, at Bluffdale City Hall, 2222 West 14400 South, Bluffdale, Utah. The meeting will begin at 6:00 PM or as soon
thereafter as possible. This meeting will also be broadcast live to the public at: www.bluffdale.com. The public may
comment at the meeting or by emailing comments to councilmeetingcomment@bluffdale.com by 4:00 PM the day of the
meeting. Emailed comments will be submitted to the City Council but will not be read at the meeting. Notice is further given
that access to this meeting by the City Council may be by electronic means.
In the event the meeting is disrupted in any way that the City in its sole discretion deems inappropriate, the City reserves the
right to immediately remove the individual(s) from the meeting and, if needed, end virtual access to the meeting. Reasons
for removing an individual or ending virtual access to the meeting include but are not limited to the posting of offensive
pictures, remarks, or making offensive statements, disrespectful statements or actions, and any other action deemed
inappropriate.
BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL WORK SESSION 6:00 P.M. (The work session is for identifying future items and other
council discussion. In accordance with Utah Code § 52-4-201(2)(a), while the meeting may be open to the public, there will
not be any opportunity for public input during the work session).
1.    Call to Order: (Roll Call).
2.    Discussion/Presentation Items:
      2.1   Presentation and discussion regarding the Salt Lake County Animal Services 2022 Annual Report for
            the City of Bluffdale. ((Presenter Carrie Sibert, Community Liaison Coordinator, SLCO Animal Services)
      2.2   Budget discussion relating to various budget and finance topics. (Staff presenters Stephanie Thayer
            and Bruce Kartchner)
3.    Council Discussion:
4.    Adjournment.
BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL REGULAR BUSINESS MEETING 7:00 P.M.
1.    Call to Order: (Roll Call, Invocation, Pledge of Allegiance*).
2.    Minute and Agenda Approval:
      2.1   March 08, 2023, City Council & RDA Combined Meeting Minutes.
      2.2   April 12, 2023, City Council, Planning Commission & RDA Combined Meeting Minutes.
      2.3   Approval of this meeting’s agenda.
3.    Mayor and Council Reports:
4.    Public Comment: (This is a time and place for any person who wishes to comment on items not scheduled on the
      agenda for public hearing. Any person or group wishing to comment on any item not otherwise scheduled for public
      hearing on the agenda may address the City Council at this point by stepping to the microphone and giving his or her
      name for the record or by emailing councilmeetingcomment@bluffdale.com. Comments should be limited to not
      more than four (4) minutes, unless additional time is authorized by the Chair. Groups wishing to comment will be



City Council Meeting – May 10, 2023                                                                                 Page 1 of 3
       asked to appoint a spokesperson. Items brought forward to the attention of the City Council will be turned over to
       staff to provide a response outside of the City Council meeting.)
5.     Presentation Items:
       5.1   Day Ranch Park Update. (Staff presenter Jennifer Robison)
       5.2   Fraud Risk Assessment – Fiscal Year 2023, City of Bluffdale. (Staff presenter Bruce Kartchner)
6.     Consent Agenda: (These items are considered by the City Council to be routine and will be enacted by a single
       motion. If discussion is desired on any particular consent item, that item may be removed from the consent agenda
       and considered separately. No public comment will be permitted.)
       6.1   None.
7.     Action or Discussion Items; Items Continued from Previous Meeting: (These items are considered by the
       City Council individually. No public comment will be permitted.)
       7.1   Resolution No. 2023-21- Selecting CMCG for Railroad Crossing and authorizing City Manager to
             execute documents. (Staff presenter Michael Fazio)
       7.2   Resolution No. 2023-19 – Consideration and adoption of a resolution of the City Council of Bluffdale,
             Utah; Ratifying the Local Building Authority of Bluffdale, Utah’s; approval and adoption of a
             Parameters Resolution authorizing the issuance and sale of not more than $16,000,000 aggregate
             principal amount of the authority’s lease revenue bonds, series 2023; and related matters. (Bruce
             Kartchner, Matt Dugdale)
       7.3   Ordinance No. 2023-09 – Amending the City of Bluffdale Alcoholic Beverage Licensing for Special Use
             Permits. (Staff presenter Bruce Kartchner)
       7.4   Ordinance No. 2023-10 – Healthy Bluffdale Coalition organization. (Staff presenter James
             Dunkelberger)
       7.5   Resolution No. 2023-20 – Appointing board members to Healthy Bluffdale Coalition Board (Mayor
             Hall)
       7.6   Budget discussion continued relating to various budget and finance topics. (Staff presenters Stephanie
             Thayer and Bruce Kartchner)
8.     Public Hearing Items: (Public comments must abide by the requirements listed above).
       8.1   None.
LOCAL BUILDING AUTHORITY OF THE CITY OF BLUFFDALE BOARD MEETING
      a. Call to Order: (Roll Call)
             i.   LBA Resolution No. 2023-02 – Consideration and adoption of a resolution of the Local Building

                  authorizing the issuance and sale of not more than $16,000,000 aggregate principal amount of its
                  lease revenue bonds, series 2023; and related matters. (Bruce Kartchner, Matt Dugdale)
      b. LBA Discussion:
      c. Adjournment.
CONTINUATION OF CITY COUNCIL BUSINESS MEETING
9.     Staff Reports, Additional Council Discussion, and Calendaring Items:
10.    Closed Meetings - if any: (This meeting will be closed to the public for one of the stated purposes found in Utah Code
       § 52-4-205(1), which is usually for one of the following purposes: discussion of the character, professional competence,

Bluffdale City
2022 Annual Report
Animal Services had been working towards No-Kill
programming since 2008, but the first official year
  achieving No-Kill was 2013. Since then, we have
   maintained No-Kill every year, saving tens of
 thousands of lives, and making 2023 an important
milestone for our agency, our community, the cities
 we are honored to serve, and the pets that enrich
                     our lives.

     Today we share with you the services and
initiatives that help make this possible. Thank you
for supporting the work that we do and for caring
      about people and pets in our community.

   livestock

   exotics

Online Licensing & Pictures

Enrichment (Agility/Training)

Adoption Counseling & Follow-Up

On-Site fully staffed operational Veterinary Clinic

Lawful Ability to:

   Provide Surgeries/Misc. Clinic Services

   Administer Vaccines to Owned Pets

   Prescribe Medicine

Outreach Programs with dedicated staff:

OFFICERS OF THE YEAR AWARDS
THESE OFFICERS HAVE GONE ABOVE AND BEYOND IN THEIR DAILY
RESPONSIBILITIES. EACH HAS SHOWN IMPECCABLE QUALITY IN THEIR
WORK AND DEDICATED SERVICE TO THE PUBLIC.
ANIMAL CARE CAMPUS GOALS
REGIONAL ADOPTION & EDUCATION
CENTER

 County Council approved funding for a
 study for a regional animal care campus!
    This facility will serve as an animal
    adoption center for animal control
    agencies.
    Provide educational trainings, interactive
    areas, and activities for the public.
    Includes a regional dog park, event venue
    space, and a variety of animal related
    resources.
MOBILE SPAY & NEUTER CLINIC
MOBILE VET CLINIC THAT WILL PROVIDE
SPAY & NEUTER SURGERIES COUNTYWIDE

The County Council approved funding
for a mobile clinic that will help assist
low income families around the valley.

   The clinic will operate for 18 months.

   Each week the mobile clinic will go to
   various neighborhoods within Salt
   Lake County and provide pet
   sterilization surgeries.
PET FOOD PANTRY
MONTHLY PET FOOD AND
SUPPLIES GIVEAWAY


Funded and supplied through donations
and grants our agency hosts a monthly
pantry to assist residents in need.

Residents who are struggling and need
assistance with pet food and supplies
can drive up and receive needed food
and supplies when available.
LICENSING
RESIDENTS ARE REQUIRED BY LAW TO LICENSE
ALL OWNED DOGS AND IN SOME JURISDICTIONS
CATS AND OTHER PETS PER ORDINANCES.


   Licensing is the only lawful way to
   prove ownership.

   Requires a valid and current rabies
   vaccination.

   Residents are able to license at the
   shelter, online, or by phone

   Our new license tags feature a QR
   code through pawfinder.com to help
   reunite lost pets with their owners

30            2.1    Discussion with Joe Wilkins Regarding Modification of Front Setbacks in the
31                   Quail Ridge I/O Zone. (Joe Wilkins).
32
33   Joe Wilkins was present requesting a modification to the front setbacks in the Quail Ridge I/O
34   Zone. Several changes had been made to the project so far as a number of unforeseen problems
35   were encountered with the layout. Three lots on the north side did not quite work because they
36   would drive out onto the main road. As a result, City Engineer, Michael Fazio suggested that there
37   be a cul-de-sac instead, which had been designed. On paper, the cul-de-sac looked good but there
38   were issues related to the canal and the hills which impede the building envelope. Mr. Wilkins




                                                     1
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   had been working with Doug Jessop from Sage Homes to design the residences but there was not
 2   enough space on the properties to accommodate what was envisioned.
 3
 4   Mr. Wilkins reported that twin homes were previously discussed because they are more affordable.
 5   Drawings were prepared but the decision was made not to rezone. To make the houses work on
 6   the property there need to be concessions on the setbacks. Currently, there are 20-foot setbacks to
 7   the back of walk but the proposal was for 20-foot setbacks for the garages and 10-foot setbacks for
 8   the homes. Mr. Wilkins understood that this was a bit tight but noted that there is nothing adjacent
 9   to the property. No one will be impacted by the smaller setbacks in the front with the exception
10   of the homeowners. There would not be any foot traffic there.
11
12   City Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell shared renderings with the Council.
13   He suggested that the Council Members visit the site to see how the grade and elevation changes
14   in the area.   Mayor Hall wondered why the twin home concept had not moved forward.
15   Mr. Wilkins clarified that the rezoning process would be longer than the term of his loan.
16   Mr. Crowell reported that a simple rezone would take a few months, assuming there are no
17   continuations. The length of the loan term was a factor. He noted that the I/O Zone is unique in
18   the community. It is an infill overlay zone that could be applied to R-1-10 and there are only a
19   few in Bluffdale. When it was created, there were a few modifications to the setbacks. The front
20   setback was 20 feet, the side setbacks were 10 feet and eight feet, and the rear setbacks were 20
21   feet. He noted that there were some accommodations made overall.
22
23   Mr. Crowell reported that what is currently being requested for the setbacks will require a text
24   amendment to the I/O Zone. The process of going through the Planning Commission and City
25   Council would still be required. Council Member Kallas asked for additional information about
26   the current zone. Mr. Crowell clarified that in this case, an overlay was applied to the R-1-10
27   Zone. It applied to the small six-lot subdivision and was an infill overlay zone for Quail Ridge.
28   Council Member Kallas believed the intention was to have single-family homes with a change to

 1   the front setback. That would move the homes further away from the slopes. It was noted that the
 2   number of homes that are proposed would not change.

 5   working with Mr. Jessop at Sage Homes. One month ago, he indicated that it was not possible to

 9   10-foot setbacks but the garage would have a 20-foot setback, as currently proposed. This was

11   the setback would help the property. Council Member Aston agreed. She also did not oppose the

21   Mark Reid believed that six units or 12 units would be appropriate in terms of traffic levels. The
22   Council acknowledged that it is a difficult piece of property to develop. Mr. Crowell explained

                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1          2.2     Healthy Bluffdale Coalition Organization and Grant Discussion. (Julia Glade
 2                  and Caroline Marino from the Salt Lake County Health Department).
 3
 4   Julia Glade and Caroline Marino from the Salt Lake County Health Department were present to
 5   discuss a grant that was awarded to Bluffdale City. Ms. Glade shared information about the
 6   Coalitions Lite grant that Bluffdale received.        She explained that Community Coalition
 7   frameworks help community members engage in a structured process to identify what is important
 8   to their community and how to work together to efficiently and effectively make an impact.
 9   Community coalitions are strategic planning processes where there is an assessment and priorities
10   and strategies identified. There are several evidence-based frameworks and structured processes
11   that guide the development and activities of a Community Coalition. Those frameworks can be
12   fairly complex, expensive, and time-consuming. As a result, Coalitions Lite was established. It
13   provides a research-based framework that is more efficient due to the shortened timeframe. This
14   is a pilot program that is being tried out in the community.
15
16   For Coalitions Lite, there is a grant and application process. For those who apply, there are
17   eligibility requirements. Ms. Glade reviewed the following requirements:
18
19         There needs to be a Community Coalition;
20         The Coalition must be formed or ready to be formed;
21         Stakeholders, partners, and key leaders must be at least aware and supportive of the
22          Coalition;
23         The Coalition must focus on an aspect of a healthy community;
24         The Coalition must have a minimum of five sectors represented and committed (i.e.
25          residents, parents, schools, local government, relevant non-profit organizations); and
26         All funding must be spent to carry out the Coalitions Lite strategic planning process.
27
28   Ms. Marino reported that $35,000 was awarded and would be paid out in two installments. One
29   would be paid out upon execution of the contract and the second in mid-June. There were also
30   reporting requirements, which were outlined in the contract. Since the money was coming through


                                                      4
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                   BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                     REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                   COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   the Salt Lake County Health Department from the State, there was a requirement to report through
 2   the State’s database. There would also be post-meeting surveys. Everyone who participates in
 3   Coalition Meetings will fill out a brief survey afterward. Since it is a pilot program, it is important
 4   to understand what is working and is not working. Bluffdale would be asked for notes and plans
 5   as well as one hour per month to discuss the process.
 6
 7   Ms. Marino presented an overview of the process and explained that there are six steps and
 8   meetings associated with each step. The first meeting would focus on the timeline at which time
 9   the assessment process would begin. There would be community discussions and data collection
10   to better understand the priorities. From there, strategies would be determined to address the
11   selected priorities. There would need to be communication about the plan so residents and other
12   stakeholders would be informed. It is a fairly straightforward strategic planning process that will
13   take four to eight months, which is less time than required by other frameworks. She noted that it
14   would be possible to walk away with clear goals and strategies.
15
16   Mayor Hall reported that the Healthy Bluffdale Coalition applied for a grant to write a Strategic
17   Plan. Based on the presentation, she assumed that Staff support would be needed since data would
18   need to be collected and there would be an education component. She asked how many meetings
19   would be needed to create a Strategic Plan. Ms. Marino responded that the framework outlines six
20   separate meetings. The Community Coalition would do a lot of the work. The Salt Lake City
21   Health Department would also be able to assist in obtaining the data and presenting it to the
22   Coalition. She stated that it would be beneficial to have a member of Staff plan and facilitate the
23   meetings. This would ensure that all the material is worked through.
24
25   Council Member Crockett reported that Healthy Bluffdale involves members of the community.
26   The initial brainstorming had been done. The grant was applied for so that a Strategic Plan could
27   be created and there would be a framework in place. Once that is established, it would be possible
28   for anyone to run the program and it could be passed on for years to come. Her concern was that




                                                       5
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   some of those who are currently involved may not want to take on the responsibilities associated
 2   with the grant.
 3
 4   Mayor Hall wondered if it was possible to take some of the grant money and use it for Staff support.
 5   City Attorney, James Dunkelberger believed there needed to be some determination about how the
 6   City can spend the money. He believed a separate body set up within the City should facilitate
 7   Healthy Bluffdale and spend the money. To determine how the money is spent, it would be best
 8   to hold a meeting and implement a more formal process.
 9
10   Mr. Reid asked for examples of the types of strategies that could come out of this type of process.
11   Ms. Marino clarified that the Coalition will determine what the focus will be. It is broad to start
12   and then there would be more specific decisions made going forward. For example, if the broad
13   area of interest is walkability, over time it would be possible to determine certain neighborhoods
14   that need attention and create strategies to address those needs. The Coalition will make the
15   determination and look at a wide range of strategies. The $35,000 would allow the Coalition to
16   move through the strategic planning process.
17
18   Mr. Reid wondered what types of projects other coalitions have done in relation to mental health.
19   Ms. Glade shared information about different coalitions she has worked with. She explained that
20   if the broad issue is mental health, it is necessary to determine what the focus should be such as
21   suicide prevention, opioid use, or mental health issues in older populations. The group would
22   determine a more specific area of focus. From there, data would be reviewed, interviews could be
23   conducted, and there would be additional discussions within the Coalition. At that point, it would
24   be possible to create a Strategic Plan to address the chosen area of need.
25
26   Council Member Aston asked about the timeline to utilize the funds. Ms. Marino clarified that the
27   contract Bluffdale would enter into with Salt Lake County would extend through December 31,
28   2023. The contract, as currently written, states that if there is additional funding, it would be
29   possible to continue working with Bluffdale for additional years on implementation of the various
30   strategies. Council Member Aston was supportive of the Coalition and moving forward.

                                     REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                   COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1
 2   Administrative Services Director, Bruce Kartchner believed the intention was to narrow down
 3   what the community as a whole wants to do. If the community is interested in something that is
 4   not currently in Bluffdale, such as a mental health clinic or similar, he wondered if that would
 5   create a conflict since the City cannot necessarily pursue that. Ms. Marino reported that at least
 6   five community sectors need to be involved in the Community Coalition. Those sectors would
 7   broadly represent the community. If the Coalition makes a decision about a desired focus, it is not
 8   the responsibility of the City to move that forward. Instead, the Coalition needs to do so. Ms.
 9   Glade noted that there are tools to assist the Facilitator and Coalition through the decision-making
10   process.
11
12   Mr. Kartchner noted that there are a number of reporting requirements involved. He suggested
13   that the City Attorney prepare a memo outlining the various requirements and the process moving
14   forward. The Council could then make a final decision about the grant. Mayor Hall pointed out
15   that there is a timeline with which to accept the grant. Ms. Marino clarified that the grant officially
16   started on February 15, 2023. Mayor Hall believed a decision needed to be made shortly. It was
17   not possible to do the work without Staff support, so it would come down to determining whether
18   a Staff Member has time to commit to the Coalition. Council Member Crockett felt it was
19   important to make sure the grant money will cover Staff time. Ms. Glade reported that in her
20   experience, coalitions often have a paid Coordinator. The funds could be used to hire and fund the
21   position. Mayor Hall expressed gratitude for the information shared.

23          2.3     Discussion with County Councilman Sheldon Stewart.
24
25   Salt Lake County Council Member, Sheldon Stewart, introduced himself and reported that he
26   represents Salt Lake County in District 5, which includes Bluffdale. The bill from the Legislative
27   Session that would have the most impact on Bluffdale is Senate Bill (“S.B.”) 260. There was an
28   attachment to that bill related to mental health. A lot of work had been done to remove that and
29   clarify language related to funding for the Utah Transit Authority (“UTA”). He referenced the 5th
30   of a 5th that the County would have to enact. When an attempt was made to pass that through a


                                                       7
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   ballot initiative, it failed in Salt Lake and Utah counties. His concern with UTA was that nothing
 2   happened in the southwest. Additionally, nothing in the southwest was added to the 10-Year Plan.
 3   Riverton Mayor, Trent Staggs, had been very vocal about that.
 4
 5   Mr. Stewart shared information about House Bill (“H.B.”) 374, which relates to the Unified Police
 6   Department (“UPD”). The reason that bill was something he supported was because the County
 7   currently utilizes shared services, however, the delivery of services does not include Bluffdale,
 8   Riverton, Herriman, or South Jordan. It all goes into UPD cities. As an example, in the Gang
 9   Unit, 799 incidences occurred in Millcreek, two in Bluffdale, five in Riverton, and eight in
10   Herriman. That was a notable disparity. Although the UPD would argue that Millcreek has a more
11   serious gang issue, he believed the issues were likely comparable to other cities. He reiterated that
12   there is a disparity in the service and numbers.
13
14   Council Member Kallas discussed the 5th of a 5th and noted that the bulk went toward UTA and
15   there is no UTA service in Bluffdale. There have been requests to be included in the plan but that
16   had not happened. Bluffdale residents pay the full tax and receive a smaller portion of their
17   contribution.
18
19   Council Member Kallas stated that there was a push to have Bluffdale represented on the Point of
20   the Mountain Board Authority. Mr. Stewart reported that Dawn Ramsey is the representative for
21   the southwest. Council Member Kallas pointed out that to reach The Point it is necessary to drive
22   through Bluffdale. The infrastructure that is coming will come in through Bluffdale, yet there is
23   no coordination. It made sense to have someone from Bluffdale on the Board. Mr. Stewart offered
24   to look more into the appointment process and report back. Council Member Crockett agreed with
25   the comments shared by Council Member Kallas. It did not make sense to her that Bluffdale does
26   not have a seat on that Board.
27
28   Mayor Hall reported that the County has land in Bluffdale and a Rec Center was part of the future
29   plans. She hoped there could be a meeting about that in the future. The Council thanked

 1      The Legislative Session has come to a close. On Saturday, there was a Town Hall at
 2       Riverton City Hall where representatives for Bluffdale, Herriman, and Riverton met to
 3       recap the session. It was an informative meeting and she thanked the Legislators for their
 4       work. More bills were put forward than in any other year in the history of the State. She
 5       was told that it was due to population increases and redistricting. There were also 15 new
 6       representatives which means more people are serving in the Legislature than ever before.

 8       Kallas indicated that the Utah League of Cities and Towns (“ULCT”) produced literature

13       is a monthly meeting involving all Salt Lake County Mayors. The most recent meeting
14       took place in Cottonwood Heights. Mayor Hall also attended the Wasatch Front Regional
15       Council (“WFRC”) Meeting for Transcom.

17       that residents read that information. The Senior Center had reached out to see if the Council
18       would be interested in serving lunch on March 17, 2023, since over 100 seniors were
19       scheduled to come for St. Patrick’s Day.

22       neighborhoods. She also thanked the Public Works Department for the time spent plowing.

 5   5.       Public Comment.
 6
 7   Bo Rich identified herself as the Quality Manager for Canyon Labs located on Bringhurst Drive.
 8   Canyon Labs is a lab testing facility and is applying for a Special Use Scientific Permit through
 9   the Department of Alcohol and Beverage Services that would allow them to purchase alcohol. To
10   receive the permit local consent is needed from the City of Bluffdale. Ms. Rich clarified that
11   alcohol will not be sold. The alcohol is for testing and cleaning purposes within the lab. Ms. Rich
12   reported that she has been working with Community Development Coordinator, Kjersti Jarman,
13   who suggested that she speak during the public comment period.
14
15   Mayor Hall reported that there is a process involved in receiving local consent.                 City
16   Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell reported that there is nothing in the Code
17   that allows the City Council to grant local consent for scientific use. It was not anticipated and did
18   not exist. As a result, something would need to be created and brought to the Council. Council
19   Member Aston suggested the Council sponsor an update to the Code. Other Council Members
20   agreed and were supportive of the scientific use. The item would come back in at a future date.
21
22   There were no further comments. The public comment period was closed.

4           7.1     Ordinance No. 2023-07 – Adopting an Amended Consolidated Fee Schedule
5                   for Various Fees Charged by the City of Bluffdale. (Staff Presenters, Stephanie
6                   Thayer and Bruce Kartchner.
7
8    Finance Manager, Stephanie Thayer reported that the Consolidated Fee Schedule was included in
 9   the packet and was used by Staff as a reference when charging fees. The intention was to cover
10   costs. At least once a year, the department heads and other members of Staff meet to review and
11   discuss the fees. The review normally takes place as part of the annual budget but is occasionally
12   done mid-year as well. She noted that there are proposed changes that were redlined in the packet.
13
14   The Engineering Department conducted a review of their fees and proposed changes to Section 4.
15   For instance, the Encroachment Permit section showed increases and there was also a proposed
16   addition of a Micro-Trenching Fee. Under the Cash Bond Criteria for Encroachment Permits
17   section, the proposal was to change the ranges and a disclaimer was added. Council Member
18   Kallas asked how the Micro-Trenching Fee would impact the proposal from Google Fiber.
19   Assistant City Engineer, Dan Tracer, clarified that it was the fee to be paid by Google Fiber who
20   was aware of the fee. The revisions were made based on their feedback.
21
22   Ms. Thayer referenced the proposed increase under the Stop Work Order section. Under the
23   Garbage section, the increases from the provider were listed. Administrative Services Director,
24   Bruce Kartchner clarified that an update is received from the garbage collection company each
25   December. The increase became effective in January. The numbers listed were reflective of the
26   rate increases that were part of the contract. Ms. Thayer reviewed the other proposed increases,
27   which included changes to the Water section of the Consolidated Fee Schedule. To address cases
28   where utility accounts had become delinquent, there was an After-Hours Re-connection fee. If
29   after 8:00 p.m. that would be considered an after-hours connection. There was also a fee for
30   Unauthorized Water Access to address that issue.
31
32   Mayor Hall opened the public hearing. There were no comments. The public hearing was closed.

 8          7.2     Resolution No. 2023-10 – Adopting a Budget Adjustment for the 2022-2023
 9                  Fiscal Year Budget. (Staff Presenters, Bruce Kartchner and Stephanie Thayer.)
10                  [RDA Public Hearing Held in Conjunction with the City Budget Hearing].
11
12   Mr. Kartchner shared information about the budget process and reported that each year, the City
13   Council is required to pass a budget in June. To accomplish that, there is a Preliminary Budget
14   that is passed in April of each year. Budgets are estimates and projections and as the year passes
15   various changes occur. In those instances, there need to be adjustments made to the budget to
16   reflect additional costs, changes in revenues, and reductions in costs. The current agenda item
17   pertained to a budget adjustment for Fiscal Year 2022-2023. A budget amendment typically takes
18   place in February but a fairly significant snowstorm resulted in the cancellation of the February
19   City Council Meeting. As a result, the item was moved to tonight’s meeting for discussion and a
20   vote. Mr. Kartchner noted that Capital Projects are part of the discussion because they can change
21   throughout the year as different costs come into play. For instance, sometimes money is shifted
22   from one year to another.
23
24   Ms. Thayer presented the Mid-Year Budget Adjustment that pertains to the fiscal year ending June
25   30, 2023. With respect to forecasting, Staff is conservative with the revenues and is precise on
26   expenditures. Occasionally, there are unforeseen circumstances that need to be accounted for. The
27   first item in the Mid-Year Budget Adjustment FY 2023 document related to City Council salaries.
28   Utah Retirement Systems (“URS”) looks at the salaries each February and based on the minimum
29   earnings to remain eligible with URS, there needs to be a $100 increase. To maintain that
30   eligibility, the City Council salaries need to increase to a total of $1,240 per month.
31

 5   8.       Action or Discussion Items or Items Continued from Previous Meeting:
 6
 7            8.1      Possible Discussion Regarding a Special Service District for Police and/or Fire.
 8                     (Staff Presenters, Bruce Kartchner, and James Dunkelberger).
 9
10   City Attorney, James Dunkelberger reported that Staff prepared information for the Council
11   outlining alternate Public Safety funding options. Rather than passing ordinances with increases
12   or having an increased budget for Public Safety from the General Fund, it was necessary to
13   consider other options. He reviewed the following two options:
14
15           Utah Code Title 10 - Special Fund or Special Levy:
16                  o Utah Code Title 10 regulated municipalities. Within Title 10, there was a section
17                     that allowed the governing body of a city to make a special levy where those levy
18                     taxes would be placed in a special fund. The special fund would be a separate line
19                     item on the budget, but would otherwise function largely within the existing
20                     structure of the city. It would be created by Resolution, drafted by the City
21                     Attorney, and brought before the City Council for consideration.
22           Special Service Area or Special Service District:
23                  o Utah Code Title 17B. A Special Service Area and Special Service District would
24                     work effectively the same way within the City from a pragmatic standpoint. Other
25                     cities, such as Riverton and Herriman, had Special Service Areas in place.
26
27   Mr. Reid clarified that a Special Service Area creates an area that is the City boundary. He noted
28   that a Special Service District typically has multiple boundaries. The functions are similar,
29   however, in this case, a Special Service District does not need to be discussed because a Special
30   Service Area would take care of everything that is needed for Bluffdale. Mr. Dunkelberger
31   reported that there had been previous discussions with the Council about Special Service Areas


                                                       20
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   and Special Service Districts. The Special Fund option was introduced at the last meeting but was
 2   not discussed in detail. Essentially, it was another option to earmark funds for public safety and
 3   remove them from the General Fund. Mr. Reid noted that there had been discussions about
 4   separate funds within the General Fund such as the Arts Council and Miss Bluffdale, which are
 5   General Fund sub-funds. The Special Levy would be a separate fund that only addresses Public
 6   Safety needs. Additional information would be shared later in the meeting.
 7
 8   Mayor Hall asked which cities have done Special Service Areas. Mr. Dunkelberger reported that
 9   Riverton has both a Law Enforcement and Fire Protection Area. There was one for each. Herriman
10   did so as well. South Salt Lake does not have an area but uses the Special Levy in a separate fund.
11   He pointed out that the South Salt Lake City Attorney had been very helpful and shared information
12   about their process.
13
14   Mr. Dunkelberger reported that both funding options would start with a resolution. If the Council
15   decides to move forward with a Special Service Area, it was recommended that one more attorney
16   be hired to serve as a Consultant for that process. There are only a few attorneys in the area who
17   have experience with the Special Service Areas. He noted that there had been correspondence with
18   one such attorney by the name of Rachel Anderson. If the Council was interested in pursuing that
19   option, Mr. Dunkelberger would do the drafting and Ms. Anderson could provide additional
20   expertise.
21
22   Mr. Dunkelberger described what would happen if a resolution was passed that the residents do
23   not support. Under Title 17, there is a specific procedure for “adequate protest.” If there is a
24   certain threshold of landowners in the district who are opposed to the resolution, the creation of
25   the district could be halted. With respect to a Title 10 Special Fund or Special Levy, there was no
26   particular statutory protest procedure but it was subject to a referendum.
27
28   In terms of organization, a Special Service Area is a separate entity. The Special Fund, however,
29   is simply a separate fund. Mr. Kartchner noted that for a Special Service Area or Special Service
30   District, there would be a separate Board and potentially separate administration. There are


                                                     21
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   additional costs associated with the Special Service Area that differ from the Special Fund. The
 2   Special Fund essentially falls under the existing organization. Mr. Dunkelberger reported that the
 3   Special Service Area Board is referred to as the Board of Trustees and there are different rules for
 4   appointments. He explained that Council Members can serve as trustees but there are rules that go
 5   along with those appointments.
 6
 7   Mr. Dunkelberger presented information regarding accounting for both options. For a Special
 8   Service Area, there would be a separate budget and audit process. The Special Fund would use
 9   existing City infrastructure. Meetings would likely be similar to what is done with the LBA and
10   RDA. The statute specifies that the Special Service Area can use City facilities but there is a
11   requirement to pay for the services and facilities. That was an area where there would be potential
12   complications to be considered. There was discussion regarding the administrative process for the
13   various funding options.
14
15   Mr. Dunkelberger reported that both options allow funds to be earmarked for a specific purpose.
16   The options are essentially two different ways to do the same thing but there is a distinction in the
17   practicalities. Mr. Kartchner shared information about Herriman that included the tax line item.
18   It showed that there is a General Fund Tax as well as two additional areas that levy their own taxes.
19   One was the Fire Service Area and the City Safety Enforcement Area, which were the Herriman
20   fire and police services. He clarified that both are Special Service Areas. Mr. Kartchner reviewed
21   information about South Salt Lake, which has the Special Fund as opposed to the Special Service
22   Area. South Salt Lake City has two line items under the City itself. It showed the General
23   Operations and the Public Safety Special Levy. The Public Safety Special Levy had to go into the
24   Public Safety Fund, which was the only place the money could be spent. He further reviewed
25   information about the example cities.
26
27   Mr. Kartchner noted that while Mr. Dunkelberger had presented two different options there are
28   actually three.    They include the Special Service Area, Special Fund, or no change.
29   Mr. Dunkelberger reported that with the Special Fund, an ordinance clearly specifies that the



                                                      22
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                  BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL AND
                                    REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  COMBINED MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, March 8, 2023

 1   additional levy was earmarked specifically for Public Safety. That could address some resident
 2   concerns. There were pros and cons for the Council to consider with each of the options presented.
 3   It would be much easier to set up and maintain the Special Fund but the Special Service Area
 4   would ensure that there is more separation.
 5
 6   Mr. Kartchner stated that the Special Fund was fairly simple to set up and could be done in a
 7   relatively short period of time. As for the Special Service Area, it was unlikely to happen with the
 8   2024 budget. Council Member Kallas wondered what would happen if the Council decided to
 9   move forward with the Special Service Area and the Resolution is passed in April. He wanted to
10   understand when it would go into effect. Mr. Kartchner explained that it would take time to create
11   the Special Service Area. Possible timelines were discussed. Council Member Kallas suggested
12   that the Special Service Area funding option not be done with the budget cycle given the possible
13   timelines. He wondered if it could be done mid-year. It was clarified that it needs to be done with
14   the budget. Mr. Kartchner noted that the City of South Salt Lake fully funds its Fire and Police
15   with a tax increase without reducing General Fund property taxes.
16
17   Council Member Aston pointed out that South Salt Lake has similar issues to Bluffdale where
18   residents want more certainty about how taxes are spent. During the referendum process, the
19   Council spoke to many residents and it seemed that there was no problem with the tax increase but
20   that there was no guarantee about where the funds would be used. As a result, different options
21   were now being explored to reassure residents that the funds will be used for Public Safety. She
22   noted that the South Salt Lake Public Safety Fund only funds wages and benefits. If the Police or
23   Fire need an increase in wages to retain employees, that would come from the fund. The fund does
24   not cover the entire department. That was something that Bluffdale could consider.
25
26   Council Member Kallas asked for additional information about Herriman. He wanted to know
27   when the Special Service Areas were created and what the circumstances were. Mr. Kartchner
28   explained that Herriman was initially part of the Unified Police Department (“UPD”) district and
29   the Unified Fire Authority (“UFA”) district. Herriman created the Special Service Area for Police

 1   first and then create the Special Service Area for Fire a year or two later. The Fire Area Funds
 2   were still being used to pay UFA but Herriman is no longer part of UPD. Mr. Dunkelberger noted
 3   that UFA works a little differently and some participants are more active than others.
 4
 5   Mr. Dunkelberger reported that information was being compiled based on various discussions with
 6   experts. Any outstanding Council Member questions could be emailed to him and he would look
 7   into them further. Council Member Kallas asked that a copy of the presentation slides be
 8   distributed to the Council.
 9
10   Council Member Aston reminded those present that something needs to change. The City will
11   have failure with the Fire Department at some point, so it is important to make a decision and move
12   forward so Bluffdale can continue to function and provide safety for residents. Council Member
13   Kallas agreed that something different needs to be done. He liked both of the ideas presented.
14   Ultimately, both would be more transparent for taxpayers since they can see where their tax dollars
15   are going. Doing nothing was not an option because that would mean that the existing challenges
16   are not being addressed. He suggested that the discussion continue at the next meeting.
17
18   Mr. Dunkelberger stated that if the Council is interested in moving forward with the Title 10 option,
19   he could start to draft that language. The first step for Title 17B would be to contact the Consultant
20   and obtain a quote. From there, he could better outline the process and timing. Council Member
21   Kallas suggested that the Consultant be contacted. Even if the Council does not pursue that option,
22   it is important to have that information. Having a quote and knowing the timeline would better
23   inform the decision that is made by the City Council.
24
25   Mr. Kartchner pointed out that the ongoing costs of maintaining a Special Service Area will be
26   higher because of the audits and additional segregation that is required. It would not just involve
27   additional Staff time but there would be out-of-pocket costs associated with the Special Service
28   Area. That was one factor for the Council to consider. Mr. Kartchner explained that the RDA did
29   not have a separate audit because it was incorporated into the City audit. Mr. Dunkelberger asked

 4   Mr. Reid noted that there were still several unknown elements. For instance, how to acquire

 6       held in the Senate Building at the State Capitol on the second Tuesday of each month.
 7       Because the project is significant to Bluffdale, she attends in person and is engaged and
 8       aware of what is taking place. She gets questions from numerous residents about what is
 9       going on, the timeline, and the plan details.       She reported that everything is well
10       documented and can be found at thepointutah.org. It shows when public meetings are
11       scheduled, what is on the agenda, and the Phase 1 plans. It was reported at the Board
12       Meeting that 30% of the demolition is completed, the majority of which takes place
13       underground. At one point they found an abandoned natural gas line that was filled with
14       hazardous material, which delayed the demolition.
15      Mayor Hall reported that she is a voting member of the Wasatch Front Regional Council’s
16       (“WFRC”) Regional Growth Commission and serves as Chair of the Council of Mayors
17       (“COM”). This particular COM Meeting was interesting because each of the members was
18       asked to give feedback on what they do for their Spring Cleanup. In Sandy, residents can
19       put anything on the street and it will be picked up. This effort costs the City about $1
20       million. Because of the Stormwater Pollution Prevention Plan (“SWPPP”), they have had
21       to move all pickup items into the street away from the gutter, which has impeded traffic.
22       Other cities are implementing green waste programs similar to Bluffdale’s, which began in
23       March. Flood control issues were also discussed.
24      Mayor Hall reported that she was honored to have been asked to throw the opening pitch
25       for Summit Academy High School.

27       graduation at Bluffdale Elementary for fifth graders. The following day some of the

 3      The City is working with Union Pacific on a box-jacking project. Box jacking is a
 4       technology that has been used in Europe for 35 years to construct tunnels under train trestles
 5       and tracks. Union Pacific’s Chief Executive Officer (“CEO”) arrived in town by train
 6       recently and invited some to meet and discuss concerns. Mayor Hall was excited to be one
 7       of the first cities in the U.S. to use box jacking.

10       Emergency Management Teams to discuss flood control. They learned more about
11       sandbags, which was fortunate timing.
12      Council Members Kallas and Hales were present the previous night at the Parry Farms
13       detention pond where sandbags were placed. She and Council Members Crockett and
14       Aston were at the Rodeo grounds filling the sandbags along with other residents. She
15       learned that sandbags should not be filled and laid down a certain way. If they touch
16       floodwater they become hazardous waste and must be disposed of in a specific manner.
17      Council Member Kallas reported that Public Works Director, Shane Paddock, has been
18       working diligently to keep things clean and organized. The retention pond began to overfill
19       recently. Although the overflow was headed toward the river and not putting residences in
20       danger, it was causing erosion. A few hundred community members acted quickly to come
21       together in a party atmosphere to form sandbag lines and build up the northwest corner of
22       the pond. They ultimately forced the water into the drainage. Mayor Hall commented on
23       being able to count on the community to show up in times of need.

12          requirements. The County manages the funding, which is for low-income households. The
13          Board does not include larger cities such as Sandy, Salt Lake City, and West Valley City,
14          as they have their own staff to manage the allocated funds. Applicants included South
15          Valley Sanctuary, The INN Between, First Step House, Odyssey House, and shelters in the

22          Bluffdale, with about 1,000 to be performed throughout the County. A newsletter will be
23          distributed where mosquito-eating fish for residents’ ponds will be available.

24   Representative Jeff Stenquist thanked the City Council for their service. He reported that it was a
25   successful Legislative Session. He reported on the bills he sponsored including an outdoor
26   recreation effort by chairing a commission that oversees outdoor recreation. He stated that they
27   are leading the nation in terms of what they do with outdoor recreation. He also ran a bill pertaining
28   to the Point of the Mountain Project. One involved structural changes to the governance of the
29   Point of the Mountain Land Authority. Another bill would create the Utah Innovation Center
30   Governor Cox held a bill signing ceremony the previous Monday. It creates a fund and a board to
31   evaluate research projects coming out of universities. Utah universities represent an investment of


                                                      13
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL, PLANNING COMMISSION,
                             AND REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                   MEETING MINUTES
                                 Wednesday, April 12, 2023

1    billions of dollars and generate a lot of intellectual property. The bill would create an innovation
2    lab housed at the Point of the Mountain site to bring together university work and the private sector
3    to commercialize university projects. Representative Stenquist reported that he joined the Point of
4    the Mountain Board in January but has been following the project for a long time as it is in his
5    current legislative district as well as his former City Council district.
6
7    Council Member Kallas commented that the Council is focusing on trails and active transportation
 8   and the governor has made it a priority. He asked what the Council can do to be more involved.
 9   Representative Stenquist stated that about one year ago they ran a bill that made a special earmark
10   out of sales tax revenue specifically for outdoor recreation. Because it was just an earmark, there
11   was no structure pertaining to how the money would be spent. The bill he ran this year added
12   structure and a process regarding how the money would be spent. A portion of the bill specified
13   that it would go to the existing Utah Outdoor Recreation Grant Program. Any city in the state can
14   apply for grants, which have a match requirement. Last year the grant program funded about $7
15   million worth of projects. The new bill will double the funding for local programs.
16
17   For primitive trails, Representative Stenquist mentioned a program through the Outdoor Adventure
18   Commission, which he chairs, to fund a broader range of projects. The Active Transportation Fund
19   established in a bill by Senator Harper, funds the creation of a network of trails around the state.
20   Local grants can be used for paved or unpaved trails, the Outdoor Adventure Commission grants
21   cover unpaved trails, and the Active Transportation Fund covers paved trails. The process for
22   receiving funds differs for each type of project with active transportation funding resembling other
23   paved road projects. Representative Stenquist was excited by how much was accomplished this
24   session in terms of funding for outdoor recreation.
25
26   Council Member Aston commented that Bluffdale has the second most linear vein of the Jordan
27   River in the State and is only 20 feet shorter than Salt Lake City’s. While Bluffdale is a small city,
28   she does not want it to be left out of decision-making as it covers key areas of the river.

 5   to have children. He was focused on leading the state in its abortion policy. He has also been

 7
 8   Mayor Hall asked about bills that did not pass that he will work on in the interim. Senator McCay
 9   expressed his belief that infrastructure funding in the State is broken as people who do not use the
10   infrastructure are taxed for its construction. He suggested that those who use the infrastructure
11   pay for it. He stated that a new project will require new infrastructure that will be paid for by
12   current residents for the benefit of new residents. It seemed that a better way to finance the
13   infrastructure would be to allow for lending that is secured by the property. Those who move in
14   then pay for that infrastructure. The Utah League of Cities and Towns (“ULCT”) believes it is
15   giving them taxing authority. Senator McCay disagreed.
16
17   Senator McCay pointed out that the wrong state flag is currently in use in Bluffdale. He addressed
18   some of the symbolism of the flag and explained that the gold fringe around the edge symbolizes
19   subjectivity to a monarchy leading to banter about the matriarchal nature of bees as Utah is the
20   Beehive State. The current state flag has contained some errors for the last 85 years. State Code
21   requires the shield be white instead of blue and the year 1847 should be on the shield not outside
22   as it was erroneously sewn on by the Daughters of the Utah Pioneers in 1922. He reported that
23   60% of the flag’s symbolism is federal, while 5% is related to Utah.
24
25   Council Member Kallas expressed support for the new flag and stated that he will be flying one at
26   his home. He asked about the new social media bill. Senator McCay stated that social media is
27   more toxic to children than smoking, so they are trying to implement similar regulations regarding
28   social media. He questioned how to protect children without infringing on the rights of adults.
29   They took a strong legislative stance as a starting point to negotiate. Social media companies
30   responded with lobbying efforts, which is bad for Utah. They proposed age verification systems,

 1   which means that all users are presumed to be underage and must prove that they are not, which
 2   leads to privacy concerns. Senator McCay stated that information is provided, often unknowingly,
 3   on social media regarding where someone lives and their interests. Spikes in suicides correlate
 4   with a spike in social media use, especially among young girls. He commended the Governor and
 5   Legislature’s strong stance on this issue and stated that social media companies are getting nervous

 8   Council Member Crockett asked about the new way to pay for infrastructure and asked if it applies
 9   just to roads. She noted that one of the biggest increases in property taxes is the school district.
10   She asked if consideration had been given to including schools in the special tax district
11   boundaries. Senator McCay stated that there had not been. The rationale has been that it
12   exacerbates the new versus old residents debate too steeply. That was the debate they engaged in
13   with the Canyons/Jordan School District split. That fight created contention in and around
14   education. He explained that it is nearly impossible to pay for a building with a 50-year lifespan
15   with a property tax that would not make housing unaffordable. Infrastructure is less permanent
16   and more directly connected to new residents. He cautioned against focusing too much on
17   boundaries and borders, eschewing the needs of neighboring communities, and fostering an us vs.
18   them mentality rather than finding ways to work together. Senator McCay reported that a high
19   school costs around $140 million to construct, which would be cost-prohibitive for new
20   development to fund.

22   Council Member Crockett agreed in principle but stated that there is greater growth in the Jordan
23   School District. The Canyons School District has less growth and does not have the same increase
24   in property tax funding. Property taxes were addressed in Bluffdale while the Jordan School
25   District experienced the largest tax increase.

 9
10            10.1   Presentation and Discussion Regarding All West Communications’ Desire to
11                   Enter into a Franchise Agreement with the City and Install Fiber Optic
12                   Communications Infrastructure in the City’s Right-of-Way. (All West
13                   Communications Team).
14
15   Mr. Fazio reported that he recently met with an engineer working with the All West
16   Communications team. That engineer introduced the possibility of All West Communications
17   providing internet service through fiber optics. He invited the team to visit with City Staff who
18   were impressed with the company. All West Communications is interested in installing fiber in
19   Bluffdale and asked for the opportunity to present their ideas to the City Council. Mr. Reid
20   clarified that the City had not reached out to All West Communications but All West
21   Communications reached out to the City. There was a desire to have a Franchise Agreement.
22
23   The All West Communications representatives present were identified as Phil Marchant and Mont
24   Flygare. Andrew Thompson from CRS Engineers was also at the meeting. Mr. Marchant
25   explained that one week ago there was a meeting with the City Engineer and City Manager to
26   discuss the proposal.     He informed the Council that the proposal was to obtain a
27   Telecommunications Franchise Agreement from the City of Bluffdale and then engineer and
28   design a fiber network throughout the entire City. It would connect to every home and business in
29   the City. All West Communications has a Franchise Agreement currently with Herriman. It made
30   sense to have the network in Bluffdale as well. The company studied different areas that needed
31   fiber.
32



                                                    19
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL, PLANNING COMMISSION,
                            AND REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  MEETING MINUTES
                                Wednesday, April 12, 2023

 1   Mr. Marchant reported that fiber optics are strands of glass that transport data. There are a lot of
 2   things that fiber can do. The All West Communications model included fiber from RCO to the
 3   customer end. There are no bottlenecks as there will be fiber all the way. He explained that there
 4   will be a device on each end to transport the information. The fiber provides high-speed internet
 5   to communities. All West Communications has been around since 1912, but the services have
 6   evolved over the years. The company has expanded to five counties in Utah and three counties in
 7   southwestern Wyoming. The expansion since the late 1990s has been fiber-related.
 8
 9   Something that sets All West Communications apart from other providers is the fact that it is full-
10   service. The company provides voice services as well as internet. There is also backhaul data
11   traffic. All West Communications is known for having outstanding customer service. It is possible
12   to call the number and speak to a real person. That is important to the company. In addition, the
13   company feels it is important to have a strong relationship with customers, cities, counties, and
14   State entities. Mr. Marchant reported that All West Communications has a good relationship with
15   UDOT as well as other counties and cities. In the case of Bluffdale, there is a desire to bring fiber
16   wherever it is needed. For instance, it could be used for SCADA systems. In addition to providing
17   internet services, All West Communications provides multi-GB broadband to residents and
18   businesses. There is up to 10 GB for businesses and up to 8 GB for residents. IPTV is also
19   provided, which is a streaming app that can be downloaded on a phone, tablet, or smart television.
20
21   Mr. Thompson shared engineering-related details and informed the Council that he is with CRS
22   Engineers, who is employed by All West Communications to assist with the civil and construction
23   aspects of the project. CRS Engineers is a 100-year-old company based in Utah. The company
24   helps with construction and coordinates with cities. He was appointed as the Construction
25   Manager representing CRS Engineers for All West Communications. There are currently three
26   cities under construction. All West Communications is interested in being a partner with Bluffdale.
27   It would be a significant project that will put some strain on the Public Works and Planning
28   Departments because to have a feed to every house there needs to be a cable in every street. That
29   being said, All West Communications will do its best to take the pressure off of the City. The


                                                      20
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL, PLANNING COMMISSION,
                             AND REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                   MEETING MINUTES
                                 Wednesday, April 12, 2023

 1   intention was to share the company information so all complaints, concerns, and questions are
 2   directed toward the company as opposed to City Staff.
 3
 4   If Bluffdale is interested in moving forward with All West Communications, the contractor for the
 5   City would be B Jackson Construction which is currently working on the North Ogden project.
 6   Mr. Thompson reiterated that this would be a large project but up to 10,000 feet of cable could be
 7   laid in a week. The intention was to be a partner with Bluffdale in all areas of the project.
 8
 9   Mr. Flygare introduced himself to the Council and explained that he is with All West
10   Communications and deals with construction and engineering development. When looking at a
11   place like Bluffdale, the City will be divided into sections. Each section also referred to as a service
12   area, has roughly 400 passings. Based on the very rough plans prepared so far, Bluffdale will have
13   18 service areas. Typically, one service area is completed at a time. The entire City does not need
14   to be built out before the first customers receive service. Once the first service area is completed,
15   the customers in that area will be able to receive service. Mr. Flygare explained that it makes sense
16   to build Herriman and Bluffdale together. He reiterated that All West Communications has a
17   strong relationship with UDOT and others.
18
19   If there is support from the City Council to move forward, it was anticipated that construction
20   could start in July. It will likely take 14 to 18 months to complete the entire City build-out. It
21   takes three to four weeks to do each service area. Mr. Marchant reported that after meeting with
22   the City Engineer and the City Manager, he sent them a Franchise Agreement. That was being
23   reviewed by the City’s legal counsel. He believed the partnership will be a win-win for both All
24   West Communications and the City of Bluffdale. It will enhance the City and ensure that every
25   home and business has the opportunity to receive fiber. He hoped the City Council would be in
26   favor of approving the Franchise Agreement.
27
28   Mayor Hall asked when the work began in Herriman. Mr. Flygare reported that Herriman is
29   currently under design. That construction work will likely start in June. There is a signed
30   Franchise Agreement already in place there, so the detailed design work was underway. Mayor

                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL, PLANNING COMMISSION,
                            AND REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  MEETING MINUTES
                                Wednesday, April 12, 2023

 1   Hall wondered what would happen if a signed Franchise Agreement is received from Bluffdale.
 2   Mr. Flygare explained that in that instance, the work in Bluffdale will start in July. Mayor Hall
 3   was concerned about the road and the impact of the installation. Mr. Fazio noted that the roads
 4   were of concern to City Staff as well. All West Communications was willing to stay in the public
 5   utility easement behind the sidewalk or within the park strip, rather than being on the road.
 6
 7   Mr. Fazio reported that in January, he had a phone call with Google Fiber, who informed him that
 8   they will not be ready to be in Bluffdale for a year or more. Mayor Hall believed Google Fiber
 9   wanted to only be on the road, whereas All West Communications was willing to be in the park
10   strips where it will not damage the streets. Mr. Flygare explained that both construction techniques
11   were available. Some cities chose the micro-trench technique that cuts into the asphalt but other
12   cities have chosen to have traditional placement instead. The latter was what is proposed in
13   Bluffdale and it focuses on a soft surface, park strip, and back of the walk.
14
15   Mayor Hall pointed out that some neighborhoods in Bluffdale are harder to get to. She wondered
16   if those neighborhoods would have the opportunity to have fiber. Mr. Flygare explained that the
17   intention was to build every single passing in the City. Council Member Kallas asked about roads
18   that are not City roads but private roads.       Mr. Flygare clarified that they would do some
19   investigation beforehand. The company will ask for permission to build there. If permission is
20   not granted they will build as far as the City will allow. For instance, to the end of the City right-
21   of-way. The intention, however, was to build into the private communities as well if there is
22   approval to do so. Mayor Hall knew that Herriman had been discussing fiber with All West
23   Communications for a long time. She wanted to know if other cities were also having discussions
24   with the company. Mr. Flygare reported that construction will start in Ogden in the next week or
25   two. Discussions were currently taking place with South Ogden.

28   most people need and costs approximately $70 per month. Council Member Gaston pointed out

 1   that there is no fiber in Bluffdale currently, so this is an important discussion for the City Council

                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL, PLANNING COMMISSION,
                            AND REDEVELOPMENT AGENCY
                                  MEETING MINUTES
                                Wednesday, April 12, 2023

 1   Mr. Fazio explained that it was still uncertain whether Google Fiber will come to Bluffdale or not.
 2   He added that Google Fiber also wants a Service Agreement rather than a Franchise Agreement.
 3   That means the company would pay the City fewer taxes than in a normal Franchise Agreement.
 4   The City typically receives 2% of the gross income but with a Service Agreement, it would be 1%.
 5   He felt that All West Communications would provide benefits that Google Fiber might not. Mr.
 6   Fazio reported that he reviewed the Franchise Agreement and it looked good to him. That being
 7   said, some comments had been sent back to make sure the City's interests are protected.
 8
 9   Mr. Reid reported that All West Communications is willing to have the standard agreement, pay
10   the standard fee, and do the standard installation. On the other hand, Google Fiber wants a special
11   contract, a lower fee, and to not connect to businesses. Council Member Kallas did not understand
12   why Google Fiber was unwilling to connect to businesses. Mr. Flygare provided additional
13   context. He explained that the business corridor is the most difficult place to build, so there is a
14   higher cost. He believed Google Fiber may be focused on the number of connections. Mayor Hall
15   recalled that during discussions with Google Fiber, it was noted that a Staff member would need
16   to be hired to oversee the project. It sounded like that would not be necessary with All West
17   Communications. Mr. Flygare explained that there are people to address complaints and issues.

22   placed on City property, the space would be leased by the company. Mr. Flygare stated that a lease
23   could be done or the piece of property could be purchased if preferred. All West Communications
24   has leases in some areas and purchased property in other areas.

4           10.3    Resolution No. 2023-15 – Interlocal Agreement with Salt Lake County for the
5                   Rose Creek Trail Connector Project. (Grant Crowell, Presenter).
6
 7   Mr. Crowell shared information about the Rose Creek Trail Connector Project. The community
 8   includes Rose Creek, which is in the northern middle section of Bluffdale. The only section of
 9   trail that is completed is Vintage Park to the South Jordan Canal. A large parcel came available
10   and the City was contacted by the County about potential active transportation projects that could
11   work there. It was suggested that the Rose Creek Trail come through there. The City was awarded
12   a grant for $850,000 from the Salt Lake County Regional Transportation Choice Fund.
13   Mr. Crowell explained that the intention was to acquire and construct a connector on the parcel.
14   The idea was also to study Rose Creek and determine how to create a trail system from 2700 West
15   and Bangerter Highway around the canal. The study would develop alternatives.
16
17   Council Member Kallas wondered if it was a walking trail or a bicycle trail concept. Mr. Crowell
18   explained that what is in Vintage Park is wide enough for multiple types of uses. As for the section
19   underneath Redwood Road, Staff would look at the area the following week. There was discussion
20   regarding that particular location. Mr. Reid explained that the connection could make it possible
21   to walk or ride a bicycle from Vintage Park or Jordan Parkway Trail to Wardle Fields. Council
22   Member Kallas thought the work was important and was grateful for the grant.
23
24   Council Member Aston moved to APPROVE Resolution No. 2023-15 – Interlocal Agreement
25   with Salt Lake County for the Rose Creek Trail Connector Project. Council Member
26   Crockett seconded the motion. Vote on Motion: Council Member Aston-Aye, Council
27   Member Kallas-Aye, Council Member Crockett-Aye, Council Member Hales-Aye, Council
28   Member Gaston-Aye. The motion passed unanimously.

10       There had been discussions about the speeds on the road and there would be mitigation
11       efforts put forward during the spring season.
12      There was flooding reported in Parry Farms from runoff from Herriman. It crosses Porter
13       Rockwell Boulevard in Herriman and comes between the Jordan Valley Water
14       Conservancy District and the electrical substation. It then crosses the Welby Jacob Canal.
15       There is a pipe that crosses the canal. It then proceeds to Redwood Road, which goes under
16       a pipe into the Parry Farms system. From there, it goes to the park. The runoff is so
17       substantial that it filled the pond within one hour. Since the runoff comes out of the hills,
18       it is full of debris and immediately clogs the outlet. That led to the water breaching the
19       sidewalk and running into the adjacent canal. The Public Works Department immediately
20       responded when called. Flooding over the sidewalk had been minimal. He was impressed
21       by the number of people who came out to assist.

                           CITY OF BLUFFDALE, UTAH

                             RESOLUTION NO. 2023-21

A RESOLUTION OF THE CITY COUNCIL OF THE CITY OF BLUFFDALE,
UTAH, SELECTING RLW/SWI JOINT VENTURE, A STRATEGIC
PARTNERSHIP OF RALPH L WADSWORTH CONSTRUCTION COMPANY,
LLC AND STACY AND WITBECK INC., AS THE CONSTRUCTION
MANAGER/GENERAL CONTRACTOR FOR PROJECT NUMBER
COB23001RD - 14600 SOUTH RAILROAD CROSSING FROM 800 WEST TO
1300 WEST.

       WHEREAS the City of Bluffdale, Utah (“City”), is undertaking a project to
construct a new 14600 South railroad crossing between 800 West and 1300 West
(“Project”);
        WHEREAS, as part of the Project, the City needs the professional assistance,
expertise, and experience of a qualified construction manager and general contractor
(“CM/GC”) to facilitate the design process and serve as construction manager and
general contractor for the Project;
       WHEREAS the City advertised a request for proposals and received proposals
from five (5) firms;
      WHEREAS a selection team consisting of the representatives from the Project
Engineer and City staff, reviewed all proposals and interviewed the five (5) firms; and
       WHEREAS it is in the best interests of the City of Bluffdale to select RLW/SWI
Joint Venture (“RLW/SWI”), a strategic partnership of Ralph L Wadsworth Construction
Company, LLC and Stacy and Witbeck Inc., as the CM/GC for the Project,


NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE CITY COUNCIL OF THE
CITY OF BLUFFDALE, UTAH, AS FOLLOWS:


        Section 1.   Selection of CM/GC. The City Council hereby selects RLW/SWI
to serve as the CM/GC for the Project. The Council hereby authorizes and directs the City
Manager to execute such documents and instruments as necessary to engage RLW/SWI
as the CM/GC.

      Section 2.      Effective Date. This Resolution shall become effective
immediately upon its passage.




PASSED, ADOPTED AND APPROVED: May 10, 2023.
